chairman of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism - (el) mr president, in the past i have often disagreed with the rapporteur on her approach to regional policy, but this time i agree with her. i do not know whether this will encourage him to continue in the same direction, but i would like to congratulate him on that. the second thing i would like to say is that we would prefer, as we asked mrs mccarthy as rapporteurs for the general regulation, that the guidelines should be annexed to the regulation. unfortunately, this was not the case and mr barnier had nothing to do with it: the matter was the responsibility of the previous commission. i say that to remind you of the position of parliament. the third point i would like to make is that, obviously, the guidelines are generally well received, as they do not depart from the observations we have made. they are a great help to the member states, and i would particularly like to stress the importance which the commission attaches to the issues of sustainable development, the increase in the number of jobs - with emphasis on equal opportunities - and transport. i am in full agreement with these points. however, i have a grievance to express as an islander that the development of the islands has been neglected. this is not the first time that this issue has been given the importance it should have, it is a story that has been going on for the last five years that i have been a member of parliament and i have raised this issue on many occasions. we will continue to give it a new lease of life, commissioner, because article 158 (1) of the treaty of amsterdam contains a provision for an integrated policy for the islands. the commission will therefore have to examine this particular question at last. the time has finally come, commissioner, to put the programmes into practice. the member states must therefore also assume their responsibilities and do their job well. as far as we are concerned, i would like to remind parliament that there is a code of conduct between parliament and the commission, which was signed in may. i am quite confident that this code will be respected and that parliament will be kept informed of all developments and details of the implementation of the programmes.
- (el) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i too would like to congratulate the rapporteur on her report, because it was a difficult and serious piece of work. there is no doubt that the structural policies and the cohesion policy of the european union are the basic tools for creating the conditions for the development and reduction of economic and social inequalities in the regions. the latter, despite the progress made, still have high rates of unemployment and unacceptable levels. to achieve the objectives of these policies, it is essential to coordinate and organise them on the basis of well-developed guidelines, which i would say are astute. let us not forget that when these policies are effective, they become tangible for the european citizen who benefits from them, who directly sees the improvement in his quality of life. we should also not forget that special attention will have to be paid to the remote and island regions of the european union because they suffer from fundamental handicaps in their economic and social development because of their geographical position. unless, of course, the commission intends to link them to the european continent by bridges or by undersea tunnels! in conclusion, i would like to stress that structural policies as a whole call for greater flexibility to adapt to changes and thus respond to the new challenges and opportunities that will arise with the advent of a new millennium, for which we all wish the best.
- (el) mr president, there is no doubt that mrs schroedter's report contains many important observations, and i congratulate her on that. but i think we should be even more concerned about the direction and results of the community's regional policy. in short, the massive, appalling unemployment, far from being alleviated, is worsening under the structural policy. the economy and agricultural areas are irreparably affected by the regional policy pursued, with dramatic consequences for rural employment and farmers' living conditions, mainly in the south. regional disparities are widening dramatically within the member states. the data in the sixth report show that there have been considerable differences in regional inequalities over the last ten years. until now, little or no attention has been paid to the enormous problems of the union's island regions, where deficiencies in infrastructure, transport, communications and energy invariably lead to desertification. all this is to be attributed both to regional policy and to the union's wider economic and social policy. the european union's peoples are strongly opposed to this dangerous and anti-social policy. unfortunately, the new guidelines are in the same direction and there is no indication that their application will make any change possible.
- (el) mr president, it is important that the guidelines make it possible to give a good direction and to increase the effectiveness of the programmes for the crucial seven-year period 2002-06, so that sustainable development, support for employment, particularly for women and young people, finally become a reality, and that the balance between economic, social and environmental policies is ensured. it is particularly important to tackle acute urban problems, to preserve employment in rural areas and, of course, to offer, as article 158 of the treaty specifically provides, equal opportunities for development to the islands of the european union, including the greek islands, which represent half of them. the union's cohesion policy must be further strengthened, because a europe with regions with huge disparities in living standards cannot be credible or sustainable.
- (el) mr president, we are once again discussing european competition policy. but what are the conditions under which this debate is taking place and what conclusions should we draw? what characterizes today's reality are gigantic mergers, concentrations and takeovers of very large companies, of a monopolistic nature, and the creation on a global level of groups of frightening power. and such a phenomenon should not occupy our debates ? - you did ? what competition policy is willing and able to introduce controls on the actions of these monopolies? some of the european industries, such as shipbuilding and repair, air transport and the steel industry, have been hit hard by the competition policy and have suffered the terrible consequences. they have lost important positions and market shares in the world market and hundreds of thousands of jobs. will this evolution ever occupy us ? the scandalous concentration of power in strategically important sectors gives multinational companies with a profit motive the economies of entire states - and of the member states of the union! in spite of this, we are still trying to weaken the public sector and we are about to make competition policy even more rigorous, by equating orders from the administration to public enterprises with state aid. on the other hand, the loss of hundreds of thousands of jobs has resulted in an explosion in unemployment. workers are facing an unprecedented assault on their labour and social rights. consumers are seeing their living standards fall, poverty is becoming more widespread and the public sector and the production base of most of the countries of the union are disorganized and disintegrating, all in the name of unbridled and destructive competition in the name of the absolutism of the market economy and the promotion of the monopolistic interests of big capital. we blame the competition policy for all this and we are totally opposed to it.
president of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism. - (el) mr president, as chairman of the committee on regional policy and transport, i would like to congratulate the commission, and in particular commissioner loyola de palacio, for reacting as they have to this event which has really shocked the whole of europe. as for us, as the committee on transport, we are ready to discuss the commission's communication on maritime safety and, naturally, we are also ready to discuss later what the legal implications of examining this communication are. that said, i would like to make a few comments. first, the commission has rightly focused its investigations on the liability of the italian classification society rina, since it will be necessary to examine whether community law has been applied. that is the starting point for our efforts. secondly, in addition to the responsibilities of the shipowners, it would be useful to examine, in such cases, those of the charterers, for example oil companies, which are also involved in the occurrence of an ecological disaster such as this, as well as in the reparation of damage. from this point of view, the reactions to the ecological catastrophe are fully justified. but this must not lead us to question the entire merchant marine, which is an important sector for the economy since it represents about a third of transport, and that is why our reactions must be measured, severe but just. i am not opposed to considering a more restrictive reform of community law. however, as other colleagues have pointed out, we must start with implementation, because there is already a legal framework - which we must not forget - at the level of the european union. this legal framework is quite advanced - at least in comparison with what is happening worldwide - and therefore the member states, under the supervision of the commission as well, must first of all seek to ensure that community law is effectively applied.
the commission is following with interest the planned construction of a nuclear power station at akuyu in turkey and recognises the importance of ensuring that the construction of this new plant meets the highest international standards of nuclear safety. according to our information, the decision on the choice of a bidder has not yet been made. the commission acknowledges that turkey has signed and ratified the nuclear safety convention and accepts that the responsibility for licensing and regulating the location, construction, commissioning, operation and decommissioning of nuclear power plants lies entirely with the turkish atomic energy authorities. the commission is not mandated to impose limits on the decisions taken by any country in terms of energy production, including nuclear power. as commissioner wallström indicated during the european parliament hearings in september 1999, the commission will in future raise the issue of nuclear safety and radiation protection in any relevant meeting with the turkish government and i am pleased to inform you that i will meet the turkish foreign minister in a few days and will certainly raise the subject. in particular, the commission is aware of public concerns regarding the seismic activity recorded in the ecemis fault area in the vicinity of the proposed power plant site. according to information provided by the international atomic energy agency, the design of the plant takes into account the possibility of earthquakes more violent than any recorded in the region and, in particular, ten times more powerful than the one recorded in adana in june 1998. significant design margins are provided to ensure safe operation of the plant in accordance with the environmental conditions of the site. the commission is also aware of the concerns about the possible intention to use the plant to produce materials for weapons. it notes that turkey has signed and ratified the treaty on the non-proliferation of nuclear weapons and has subsequently concluded a comprehensive safeguards agreement with the international atomic energy agency.
- thank you for your answer. i would like to make the following remarks: turkey is now a candidate country. with this project, it aims to increase its energy potential by 2%. at the same time, it is seeking to acquire from canada reactors from the candu chain, which, as it turns out, have enabled pakistan and india to acquire nuclear weapons. in this context, there is a great risk that some foolish person will consider that the new geostrategic balances in the caucasus require a neighbouring country to acquire nuclear technology of a military nature. that's the political part. the technical aspect now: the factor of safety in structures of this kind - and i speak as an engineer - has nothing to do with a level that is 10 to 20 times higher than that which is capable of withstanding a collapse. in such cases, where there is uncertainty, model tests are used. but you can't use nuclear power models. therefore, all high seismic risk areas must be excluded, or are excluded a priori, for nuclear installations such as these. the european union and the commission now have different means of action vis-à-vis turkey, and it would be good if they addressed certain issues concerning both the security of the whole region and the peaceful development of turkey within the union.
the commission has already made a number of recommendations to greece in the 1999 package of employment measures, with a view to improving the efficiency of these measures. the most important recommendations are the need to increase efforts to reform public services where there is a problem, to upgrade the statistical monitoring system and to implement prevention policies in line with guidelines 1 and 2 of the 'employment package'. it should be noted that the greek government, in the context of the implementation of the policy to combat unemployment, had two concrete programmes in 1999: "yes to working life" and "back to work". for these two programmes we do not yet have definitive results which would enable us to see whether the quantitative objectives have been met. the greek government now admits that there is a problem of not being able to record the inflows and outflows of human potential in the labour market, and that is why it has committed itself to: a) reorganizing the country's public services responsible for employment; b) reorganizing the public services responsible for employment; c) reorganizing the public services responsible for employment; d) reorganizing the public services responsible for employment; e) reorganizing the public services responsible for employment. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the employment of young people is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies, and that the employment of young people is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. in the new programme which has been submitted for the period 2000-06, and which is financed by the social fund, the resources and policies will have to be devoted, with the support of the commission, to the achievement of the objectives i have just mentioned. the commission will monitor the greek government closely to ensure that it respects its commitments.

the commission is aware of the fact that the equal initiative is not limited to certain groups of people. it concerns the fight against discrimination in the field of employment. the initiative was adopted at the berlin summit, and it was then that it was decided to take into account and integrate the professional groups of asylum seekers into its actions. i mention it as an important element of this initiative. the programme of the community initiative in each member state must comply with the regulations, which are identical to the social fund regulations, i.e. equal is subject to the latter's regulations. the programme has been submitted to parliament and we expect its opinion next month. i would point out that the equal initiative responds to national needs and projects which are defined in accordance with the strategy agreed at european level. it is the member states, the national governments, who are primarily responsible for setting up the joint bodies, submitting the proposals and choosing those who will implement the programmes. within the european commission, there is a directorate-general for employment which is responsible for implementation, and financial control is exercised by the directorate-general for financial control, the anti-fraud coordination unit and the european court of auditors. the last question concerns the amount allocated. greece received ecu 98 million with an obligation to contribute 80%, while for the island regions, and especially for the decentralized greek islands, the initiative's contribution reached 85% with a corresponding share of 15%.

counsel. - (pt) mr president, the council wishes to state that turkey has signed the convention on nuclear safety, the objectives of which are in line with the concerns expressed by the honourable member. the purpose of the convention, which entered into force on 24 october 1996, is precisely to establish and maintain a high level of nuclear safety at world level through national measures and international cooperation. it also aims to establish and maintain protection of nuclear installations against potential radiological risks in order to protect people, society and the environment from the harmful effects of ionising radiation that may be emitted by such installations. as you know, the convention also covers the prevention of accidents with radiological consequences and the mitigation of these consequences in the event of such accidents. i would also point out to the honourable member that turkey, as a candidate country for accession to the european union, will sooner or later - and i think this is an important point - have to adopt a set of policies within its own pre-accession strategy in order to be able to accept in due time the entire acquis communautaire, including therefore all the provisions in force in the field of nuclear safety.

counsel. - (pt) madam president, the question you are asking is of the utmost importance and we discussed it at length at the last intergovernmental conference. i would like to say this: transparency does not only work from the community institutions 'outwards', it also works 'between' the community institutions. i mean that there are elements of the form and the process of functioning of the community institutions which are not accessible to the other institutions. this is not only true of the commission vis-à-vis parliament, but also of the commission vis-à-vis the council. this question has been raised and discussed on several occasions. the european parliament will be able to participate in the preparatory group for the new intergovernmental conference. it's an open question. the representation and the genuine transparency of the functioning of the institutions at european level is a matter of the utmost importance which, in my view, needs to be analysed once again, particularly in the context of the next intergovernmental conference. we have no doubt about that and we believe that the members who will be taking part in this preparatory group will be able to raise this issue once again. i think that the commission's proposal must include elements which allow for greater transparency as to how the various institutions operate, but i think that there is always room for improvement in this area. we are prepared to consider any proposals that may be made in this area.
counsel. - (pt) madam president, we must all be aware of one thing: between the council and parliament, between the members of the council and the members of this parliament, there is not necessarily a gap as regards the advantages and disadvantages of transparency. we have a tendency to think that the council is the place of opacity and that parliament is the place of transparency. that's not true, man. we share your concern, madam president, about the way in which the institutions operate and how this is reflected in the outside world. we share the same concerns and we share the same reading of principles. we may differ, madam member, on the interpretation to be given to the exact way in which the transparency process may or may not work in relation to genuine transparency. the idea that the distribution of a specific type of documentation and the transparent operation of certain institutions is a factor that works in favour of the democracy of these same institutions is often a dangerous idea because, in reality, this is not the case. we know - and i will not go into this at length - that when transparency goes beyond certain limits, we are systematically put on the field of controversy and corridor negotiations. there is a general balance in the question of transparency, a balance which lies exactly between realism and demagogy.

- (el) ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, we often hear that europe has succeeded in ensuring financial stability, and that is true if we look at the public sector. but if we include the private sector, then we find that this so-called financial stability has not only not been achieved, but that we are probably sitting on a volcano ready to explode at any moment. the over-indebtedness of the private sector and the corresponding excessive rise in share prices - the two main indicators of financial market stability - have long been in the red, as the commission's own report on the economic situation in europe demonstrates in an undeniable way. the reason why the public accepts such prices without abandoning financial transactions in panic may be due to the overly optimistic expectation of future profits. speculative activities are growing beyond measure and in turn fuelling profits that will one day prove to be fictitious. excessive optimism will be replaced by disillusionment and fear, resulting in a stock market crash and eventually an economic meltdown. the risk is too great to ignore. we have to face it, even if it seems difficult. modern economic science has given us a remarkable tool with the proposal of tobin and others that we could use if we had the political will to start acting. that is what we are lacking, commissioner, the political will to start tackling speculation. but the complacent admiration or resignation of the market forces, the professions of faith of the liberals - and i regret that mr gasòliba i böhm is no longer with us - cannot continue indefinitely to characterize democracy, because they lead us straight to catastrophe!
in writing. - (el) it is welcome that the question of taxation of international capital movements has finally been the subject of a debate and decision in the european parliament. it was an initiative of our group of the european united left, which was subsequently supported by other groups. the problem is significant when one considers that every day, global transactions in financial markets amount to $1.8 trillion, while annual trade in goods and services is in the order of $600 billion, the equivalent of four days of stock market speculation. president delors was right to compare the world economy to a casino. it is encouraging that the finnish and canadian parliaments have declared themselves in favour of capital taxation, as has the un convention on the law of treaties. the commission must put forward a proposal for a tax on speculative short-term capital movements, such as the tobin tax, and the portuguese and french presidencies must make this a priority. such a tax, at a low but gradual rate, must be designed so as not to have an adverse effect on the real economy - trade and investment - and to free up funds for education, the environment or development. this measure must be a first step towards the establishment of a regulatory and taxation requirement which will make it possible to tackle the gigantic growth of international speculation. i voted for the joint motion for a resolution and it is regrettable that the christian democrats and the liberals did not allow it to be adopted.

the rapporteur. - (el) mr president, i should like to thank the portuguese presidency and the commission in advance for all the very useful contacts that have been established during this period. let me also thank my co-rapporteur, mr leinen, once again for his excellent cooperation. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the intergovernmental conference which is about to begin is important for the forthcoming enlargement in every respect. its work will provide both the structure and the method on which europe will operate in the 21st century. in order for the european union of tomorrow to function more efficiently, more democratically and in a completely transparent way, it is clear that a wide-ranging and deep reform of the institutions of the european union and of its working methods is required. the first essential condition for the success of this reform is the agenda on the basis of which the work of the intergovernmental conference will begin. it is clear from the decisions of the helsinki summit that the agreed agenda is far from satisfactory and does not guarantee the changes which are indispensable and essential to the creation of a more functional, more democratic and more transparent europe. and that is because this agenda is limited to only three subjects which only partially concern two of the institutional bodies of the european union. i shall not repeat them, since they are well known. this is justified on the one hand by the constant demand, sometimes very strongly expressed by european citizens, and on the other by the enormous scale of the great undertaking which the european union has already embarked upon, that of enlargement. with this in mind, the new intergovernmental conference cannot but include on its agenda subjects whose treatment would guarantee the smooth functioning of all and not just some of the institutions of the european union. it should also cover subjects which affect everyday life and are therefore obvious to the european citizen. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament, after many discussions and in the light of the many reports which have been drawn up so far, has openly supported the convening of the intergovernmental conference. at the same time, however, i ask that subjects which improve and complete the reform be included, and i repeat, of all the institutional bodies. but we should also add topics which directly concern european citizens. for example, health, energy, culture, transport, even tourism. on the one hand, the question of whether the extension of the agenda will be carried out is still under discussion. and at this point i would really like to emphasize the political conduct of the portuguese presidency, which has repeatedly pledged before the european parliament to make every effort in this direction. on the other hand, the question of increasing the number of subjects for discussion in no way diminishes the importance attached by each member state to the intergovernmental conference because the institutional framework on the basis of which europe will evolve in the future is the fundamental principle of european integration and is therefore of paramount national importance for each member state of the union. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i ask the house, having taken note of these facts, to approve the opinion that mr leinen and i have tabled before you so that the work of the intergovernmental conference can begin on 14 february and our two representatives at the intergovernmental conference, mr brok and mr tsatsos, can, in accordance with the opinion of the european parliament, undertake the difficult work ahead of them. the same applies to the european commission, and i would like to thank commissioner barnier once again for his contacts with the european parliament and for the very constructive proposals he has put before us.
- (el) mr president, i warmly recommend the adoption of the resolution dimitrakopoulos/leinen. this clearly expresses not only our disappointment at the inadequacy of the agenda but also our desire to see the intergovernmental conference held as soon as possible. this is also the result of the favourable timetable we have decided on. i believe that under the present rules of the treaties the european parliament will not gain much from a policy of antagonism. we must concentrate on drawing up convincing proposals for the necessary reforms in cooperation with the commission, whose proposals are remarkable. we will have to use dialogue at all political levels, including of course national parliaments. we will thus create a favourable climate for cooperation in quality reform. parliament's representatives at the intergovernmental conference will have to make it clear to the negotiating partners that with the institutional changes in the union, we are faced with the responsibility for the constitutional evolution of the union. this will require them to realise that such questions cannot be settled by the mere logic of bargaining. the subjects of this intergovernmental conference are particularly sensitive. it may be very easy to establish a number of solutions, but the question is how many of these solutions will make their way through history. i would like to highlight two potential points of tension. the first is the balance which has been maintained up to now between large and small states. europe is not and will never be a conventional federal state. the solutions we choose must be in line with the fundamental logic of the european union, which is the union of states and peoples. the other terrain of conflict is more complex. we demand that the european union of tomorrow, in view of the imminent increase in its membership, should be more efficient in its organs. this makes them easier and faster to operate. the european union, however, is, and will remain, by its very nature, complex and composed of pluri-state institutions. the possibilities for abbreviation and simplification are real. of course, these possibilities exist, but they are not unlimited. if these limits are exceeded, because of unilateral efficiency, the basis on which the legitimacy of the european union rests will be shaken. i remain optimistic, however.

in writing. - (el) our position on leader is based on a more global assessment of the general eu agricultural policy. the leader 1 programme, like the leader 2 programme, which was set up in the 1990s, was a basic element of the new cap, which emerged after the 1992 revision, and together with other individual measures constituted the so-called second pillar of the european union's rural policy growth. their real objectives were to mitigate and conceal the catastrophic consequences of the new cap and to confuse small and medium-sized farmers, not regional growth of rural areas or the preservation of the rural population, as they hypocritically proclaimed in their objectives. this can be seen from the fact that the regions where these initiatives were applied experienced a very rapid decline in agricultural income and employment, which contributed to the intensification of depopulation. a typical example is greece, which, as an objective 1 country, has fully integrated into the leader community initiatives and has already experienced an average annual decline in agricultural employment of 2.3% and a decline in agricultural income of 15.2% over the period 1994-99. we believe that leader+ will be less effective than leader 1 and leader 2 for the following reasons. the actual objectives of leader+ are the same as for leader 1 and 2. mitigate and conceal the negative consequences of the cap which will be implemented in the context of agenda 2000 and the wto. however, this cap is worse than the previous one and starts from a more unfavourable base since the revision of the cap in 1992 and the gatt agreement in 1995 created major problems and deadlocks for the agricultural economy. the selection criteria and the activities subsidised under leader at best mitigate some of the problems of rural areas of secondary importance, while at worst they degenerate into a weakening of public relations and a corruption of consciences. however, they are in no way integrated growth programmes for selected regions and do not create permanent jobs in rural areas, because most activities are not productive. the actual funds available for leader+ are less than for leader 2, despite their increase by 15% (from eur 1775 million for leader 2 to eur 2020 million for leader+). this is because the 15% increase is nominal and not real, since the average annual inflation in the community in recent years has been approximately 2%. the duration of leader+ is one year longer than that of leader 2. all eu regions can participate in the leader+ programme, whereas for leader 2 it was only the regions eligible under objective 1 and some regions under objectives 5b and 6. we point out, however, that even if the actual funds were increased for leader+, the community initiative would again have proved ineffective, because the anti-agricultural character of the cap which will be applied in the context of agenda 2000 and the wto cannot be compensated or mitigated by such types of programmes whose conclusion for many regions is concealment, disorientation and corruption of consciences. we disagree with many of the estimates in this report. we would point out once again the negative changes in the cap (1992-agenda 2000). we believe that leader+ will not substantially solve the problems of the countryside and is characterised by the stagnation of agriculture, which is and must remain the basic socioeconomic sector of rural areas. we will inform farmers of the purpose of these programmes. we will do our best to highlight them and to prevent them from being squandered. first of all, we will try to advance the struggle of farmers against the cap, this catastrophic policy which exterminates them, leads them to economic stagnation and causes depopulation of the countryside. because if the cap remains as it is, no programme will be able to ensure the survival of small and mediumsized farmers and the socioeconomic regeneration of the countryside.
commission - mr president, the commission is aware of the very difficult situation in kosovo in terms of health and health care facilities, not only for pregnant women but in general. this is a result of the recent conflict and the years of neglect and lack of maintenance that preceded it. the statistics on infant mortality are self-explanatory and completely unacceptable. however, the situation is not much better for the rest of the population. the commission's response was as follows: firstly, the health sector has been a major beneficiary of echo funding. currently, echo is focusing on the provision of medical and emergency care supplies, support to institutions and vaccination. however, there is a growing effort to establish an autonomous health care system in the province. unmik has already taken on a significant role in this area. secondly, as part of the reconstruction programme, an urgent intervention at the mitrovica hospital has already been initiated through a rehabilitation programme with a budget of one million euros. progress was slow, because of strained relations between the various ethnic groups in this divided city. however, the commission is continuing its efforts to support this project, with the assistance of unmik. we hope that one day this project can become a symbol of the benefits of interethnic reconciliation. according to the damage assessment carried out by the european community, the costs of rebuilding the health care facilities and providing equipment to the clinics and care institutions are estimated at eur 4 million. the commission is now examining the programming of the funds for the year 2000. we plan to allocate a considerable amount of money to improving the health care system. the focus will be on long-term reforms, covering sector financing as well as training and procurement. work has already begun with unmik to establish an appropriate commission contribution to this undertaking.

commission. - like the honourable member, the commission welcomes the decision taken by the heads of state or government to launch the process of drafting a charter of fundamental rights of the european union. the heads of state or government themselves, in the conclusions of the cologne european council, laid down the broad lines of the content of the charter. according to these conclusions, the charter should contain three categories of rights: the rights of liberty, equality and procedure, as guaranteed by the european convention on human rights, the rights reserved to citizens of the union and the economic and social rights, as set out in the european social charter and the community charter of the fundamental social rights of workers, in so far as they do not justify solely objectives for the union's action. the commission considers that the convention responsible for drawing up the charter of fundamental rights must respect the mandate given to it by the cologne european council. however, this does not mean that the convention should be limited to a codifying role of simply compiling existing rights from different sources. as i have already said before this parliament, the commission is convinced that the convention must be able, if necessary, to adapt and develop these rights taking account of current circumstances, in particular technological and social developments. however, given the mandate and the ultimate objective of the text to be presented to the heads of state or government, this power must be exercised with caution and on a case-by-case basis. the rights conferred by this charter will have at least two types of beneficiaries. some will apply to anyone present in the territory of the union, others will be available only to citizens of the union. it could also be envisaged that certain economic and social rights are guaranteed only to citizens of the union and to third-country nationals who fulfil certain conditions. it is not possible at this early stage in the charter's development process to prejudge its content in any more detail. it can, however, be said that the principle of equal opportunities and equal treatment between men and women, among others, as it is already one of the rights guaranteed by the ec treaty and by secondary law and as it is undoubtedly a common constitutional tradition of the member states, will certainly be one of the rights guaranteed by the future charter of fundamental rights of the union. the commission, as i have already said before this parliament, is in favour of the charter being incorporated into the treaty. however, it is fully aware that the final decision in this matter will be taken by the heads of state or government. the convention's difficult task is therefore to develop an ambitious and politically significant text which can be incorporated into the treaties as it stands. in its opinion on the convening of an intergovernmental conference to amend the treaties, the commission recalls that the european council will have to decide in the course of 2000 on the inclusion of certain subjects on the igc agenda, in particular the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. - (el) mr president, i too would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs cederschiöld, on her very serious work. it clearly outlined the problems arising from the approval of the world intellectual property organisation treaties. the approval of these is undoubtedly a historic event, as the european union is participating as a contracting party to an international treaty governing intellectual property for the first time. the road has been long, mainly because of the legal problem which arose as to the extent to which the community could be considered to have full competence in intellectual property matters. the problem has been overcome. we are called upon today to agree on the content of two treaties: the copyright treaty and the treaty on interpretation and performance and on phonograms. in the first case, it appears to respond effectively to the constant demands of authors of artistic and literary works, while at the same time creating a framework for the protection of the dissemination, commercial rental and presentation of their works to the public. as regards the second treaty on performances and phonograms, it should be noted that, for the first time, an international treaty signed by the community recognizes the exclusive rights of performers, interpreters and artists to reproduce, broadcast, rent commercially to the public and the right to fair remuneration for radio broadcasts. in this context, it is worth noting the major step forward taken by the treaty on the issue of reproductive rights. although, as the commission points out, the texts of the treaties do not contain specific provisions which deal with all the issues raised by technological developments in the various fields, it can nevertheless be said that, with the legal framework thus created, artists, performers or performers enjoy a wider protection than that which derives from the treaty of rome or even from community directives. the interpretation of article 7 of the treaty leads to the conclusion that the implementation of protection of reproduction means not only protection of the reproduction of performances or performances as such, which are incorporated in phonograms, but also protection of copying, whether partial or total, permanent or temporary, as well as protection of any other form of production. but it is also worth mentioning some points which will have to be the focus of the work of wipo or other diplomatic conferences in the future. this is primarily the right of broadcasting, as set out in article 8 (1). the possibility afforded to states, under the next paragraph of the same article, to regulate the question of the exercise of this right is dangerous and constitutes one of the most negative aspects of the treaty, since nothing prevents states from introducing special conditions for the exercise of this right, which will have negative effects on artists. as regards the right to commercial rental, the interpretation of article 9 leads us to conclude that artists, performers and entertainers enjoy this exclusive right only if it is provided for by national legislation, and i believe that there is a divergence with article 13 on this point. in conclusion, i would like to say that the ratification of these treaties opens up a new horizon for property rights. in the twenty-first century, the century of knowledge and the immaterial economy, intellectual property will be the dominant form of property and in many cases more important than traditional property. we are at the beginning of a new era of lawmaking, in which the right to exploit intellectual property will coincide very closely with the public interest in its dissemination and use by the general public.

in writing. - (el) reading the commission's communication on social protection, which is full of such good intentions, one wonders whether its authors and european workers live on the same continent, in the same social reality. the question is also whether this 'concerted strategy of modernisation' is not, in its formulation, another subtle way of carrying out a coordinated and large-scale offensive against social protection, security and the acquired rights of workers. we share many of the rapporteur's observations, but we do not share his optimism about the intentions and plans of the commission and the council in the field of social protection. the broad guidelines of the economic policy implemented; the stability pact and rigorous convergence programmes which have a severe impact on employment and promote employability and flexibility in labour relations and organisation; decisions of the commission and the ecb aimed at controlling wage and salary increases; the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster is a good example of the need to ensure that the environment is protected. it is only intended to prepare the ground by creating the conditions for a reduction in public expenditure on social insurance and a relaxation of social protection itself. when, in the mouths of many leaders, social security is repeatedly referred to as a 'cost' and an 'obstacle' to increased competitiveness, and when ways are sought of transferring this cost from firms to workers - and always, of course, under the pretext of creating jobs - we would be very surprised to see the current model of the unbridled market economy and the cold financial approach adopted towards any model, totally subject to the interests of monopolies, take under its wing the fundamental right of citizens to social protection and health care. the aim is clearly to shift from existing social security schemes - which are under state control - to private insurance schemes, which will mean new profits for capital and additional burdens for workers, who, apart from a higher cost, will "benefit" from ever-decreasing social benefits. the publicity surrounding unfair competition between public and private social security schemes and between the schemes of the member states, far from being an argument, reveals the political motives and the unbearable pressures on social security schemes to reduce or even dismantle them in favour of the private sector and the law of profit. it is well known that the quality of social security schemes depends on the level and quality of employment. how can we talk about sound and economically viable social security systems when we have millions of unemployed, poor, excluded, part-time 'poor workers', workers disorganized by the established working conditions? social protection is one of the major achievements of workers in europe, won after years of struggle against the designs of big business and thanks to the constant demonstration of solidarity that characterizes workers. today, in the europe of unemployment and underemployment, it is imperative to ensure wider social protection and to strengthen workers' rights, but this calls for another system of economic and social development which the european union, by its nature and character, is incapable of forging. on the basis of these assessments, we wish to express our radical opposition to the 'modernization' of the social protection system which the commission is seeking to introduce with its communication and to express our refusal to vote in favour of the report of the committee on employment under consideration.

counsel. - (pt) mr president, the question raised by mr karamanou is an extremely serious one, it must be said. we are fully aware of the difficult and dramatic humanitarian situation in kosovo and the problem of the displaced persons in the region from kosovo, with the consequences for children's health and mortality rates. it is clear to me that the european union has clearly contributed to the humanitarian aid efforts of the international community. as you know, the european community has given a total of 378 million euros to the province of kosovo alone, to which must be added the national contributions of the individual member states. this contribution covers various projects in the fields of health and the health system, as well as water sanitation. in addition, as you know, the european union contributes to the united nations high commissioner for refugees, which amounted to eur 66.3 million in 1999. i think the honourable member is aware that, given the necessary division of labour between international donors, the health sector in kosovo is the responsibility of the world health organisation. the organization is well aware, from the reports it has produced, of the alarming situation concerning the health of children. she concentrated her efforts on improving basic conditions in maternity wards and hospitals. however, we are aware that this situation is far from being resolved satisfactorily; that it has a dynamic and a dimension which also has repercussions in other areas of health, and more particularly in children's health. it is a political situation, not a purely technical one, but one which concerns the solution of the political situation, because there are dimensions here, which should not be hidden either, which are reflected in the fact that, until now, the international community has been unable to put into practice the functioning of a multiethnic society in kosovo, which has had adverse effects on the improvement of the health system in that region.
- (el) i thank you, mr president-in-office, for your reply which complements what commissioner patten said yesterday on this subject. however, mr president, the amount of €378 million, which you say was allocated in 1999, has in fact proved to be quite insufficient to create basic human conditions in kosovo, particularly as regards the provision of health services. do you not think, mr president-in-office, that the mere fact that infant mortality has increased is a disgrace for the european continent, a shame which should mobilize us all so that the aid we give is more effective and generous? the situation was not idyllic before the bombing, but it has deteriorated considerably since the nato intervention in terms of the natural and human environment. in these circumstances, mr president-in-office, is it not a matter of moral order that we should strive to create humane conditions for the people of kosovo, who are so sorely tried, and above all to keep all the promises we have made?
counsel. - (pt) mr president, the question put by mr alexandros alavanos is one which calls for a broad reflection. the council has already expressed on several occasions its conviction that the use of nato's tough measures was necessary and justified in order to deal with extremist and irresponsible actions which clearly violate un security council resolutions. i understand that there may be different interpretations of nato's action and the legitimacy of that action. everyone is free to make up their own mind about the facts. in my country, the different parliamentary groups had different positions. things were what they were. in any event, the council's position is clear: the aim of the north atlantic treaty organisation air campaign in the federal republic of yugoslavia was to put an end to the humanitarian catastrophe caused by the belgrade regime in kosovo. this objective was fully expressed formally in the position adopted on 8 april by the general affairs council in luxembourg. it must be said that nato's measures were not taken lightly and were implemented as a last resort, when it became clear that the possibilities of a negotiated solution were exhausted and that the belgrade authorities were concentrating their forces to drive the kosovo albanians from their lands. the honourable member is certainly aware that the prosecutor, mrs del ponte, during her recent visit to nato headquarters, stressed that the international tribunal was obliged to examine all complaints, including those against nato, including those by canadian pacifist groups. this mandatory examination will take place as charges are brought. however, i have a statement from the prosecutor that says that nato is not currently under investigation by the office of the prosecutor of the international criminal tribunal for the former yugoslavia and that nato's actions in the kosovo conflict are not under any formal investigation process. all information received over the past six months from individuals and groups requesting an investigation into nato's actions during the kosovo conflict has been recorded by the prosecutor. any other information will, of course, be analyzed by the prosecutor, who will then decide whether or not to take action. for the time being, the prosecutor has decided not to rule on any of these nato matters on a criminal level.
) (el) i thank the president-in-office, but i do not agree with him when he says that the nato intervention was intended to put an end to a humanitarian catastrophe, because we still have a humanitarian catastrophe today in kosovo where, after the hell of milosevic, the hell of the uck and nato troops follows. and as the president-in-office can see, almost half the questions he has to answer today are about kosovo. nevertheless, the president-in-office himself has admitted that referral of nato to the international criminal tribunal for war crimes is possible, following appeals by canadian and other peace organizations. and i asked the president-in-office a question to which he did not answer: if this investigation procedure were to be launched, in which the main or, if you prefer, one of the main persons examined and possibly guilty would be mr solana, who is now the high representative for the common foreign and security policy of the european union, should not mr solana be suspended from office, if that happens - and the prosecutor-general of the court seems to favour the opening of this procedure - at least until he is cleared of all charges?
counsel. - (pt) mr president, as i said on a previous question, the situation in kosovo is one to which the general affairs council on monday was sensitive. the council stressed its support for the efforts of unemic and the representative of the secretary-general, mr bernard kouchner, in particular for the creation of a provisional administrative structure in kosovo in which the guarantee of the presence of all forces, especially those which have refused to be part of this provisional structure until now, would not be a vain word. the high representative of the union for foreign affairs and common security policy, mr javier solana, presented an assessment of the situation to the general affairs council; the commission is therefore proposing to the council that the council should take the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and that the council should be able to make a decision on the matter. the forces involved have not been able to prevent this drift so far. that is why, mr president, it goes without saying that the council of ministers is seriously concerned about the current situation in kosovo and in particular about the exodus of the serbian population and other ethnic and religious minorities from kosovo (these minorities are not mentioned enough when we talk about the discrimination in kosovo). the council is appalled and concerned by the continuing level of inter-ethnic violence. as i have already said on another matter, the new outbreak of violence in mitrovica is just one example of the difficulty in overcoming the mistrust that has accumulated over the decades. of course we are only at one stage of what is a process that has had several stages in the past and i believe we must always consider it in the light of past history. the council of ministers of the european union has on several occasions demonstrated its commitment to the creation of a democratic and multiethnic kosovo, in line with the one established in un security council resolution 1244. in particular, the desirable return of all refugees, unhindered and safe. for us, mr president, i would like to make it clear that despite all the doubts that may arise from resolution 1244, it remains a central element of our approach to the region. as regards violations of this resolution, i would like to say that the council condemns all acts of violence, persecution and intolerance, wherever they come from. in order to respond to the current situation, the council strongly supported the establishment of the un mission in kosovo, unmik; it contributed to its budget and assumed responsibility for what is known as the 'fourth pillar of unmik', which concerns reconstruction and economic rehabilitation. in assessing the progress made so far by unmik, account should be taken of the extremely difficult situation of its framework of action, i.e. the high number of refugees and displaced persons who have had to be resettled, the destruction of most of the infrastructure, the still active radicalization in all sectors of the kosovo population, the collapse of the economy, the total absence of domestic revenues and the implosion of administrative structures at the local and district level, as well as the judicial and police system, the results of the war and a decade of neglect after the abolition of kosovo's autonomy by belgrade. this is an important point, and it must be clear to everyone. the change of status of kosovo within the republic of yugoslavia is at the origin of the increase in ethnic tensions which underlie the present situation. we must not forget this, so that we do not forget the past actions of the historical leaders. in these circumstances, i believe that unmik, together with the other international bodies and organisations involved, has achieved results in terms of certain objectives set out in resolution 1244, although we are still far from a satisfactory situation. the council still supports the decision of the special representative, mr bernard kouchner, to set up - as i have said - the joint interim administrative council, which should involve all the ethnic dimensions of kosovo. the european union has called on the serbs of kosovo to reconsider their boycott of the structure in order to contribute to a multiethnic zone and secure a place in the emerging administrative structures. in addition, in order to combat multiethnic violence and create the necessary security environment to prevent non-albanian ethnic kosovars from leaving the province, the european union continues to support the efforts of the unmik to rebuild the kosovo police force. despite a substantial contribution, it must be noted that so far the member states of the european union have not yet seconded officers to these police forces, which has proved to be profoundly negative as regards the functioning of a public security structure. as i said, in accordance with the conclusions of the general affairs council of 24 january, the member states will endeavour to find additional police personnel. the general affairs council reiterated this position. strengthening the police force is also an important task in the context of the growing threat of organised crime. the commission intends to include the fight against organised crime in its programme of assistance to kosovo for 2000 and invites the member states to identify the appropriate assistance they can provide in this area. we believe that a large part of the solution to the kosovo question - there is no doubt about that - also lies in the problem of stabilizing the surrounding countries. without this it will not be possible to solve the problems of kosovo. without a stabilisation of the political situation in albania - there are proposals for the commission to assess the possibility of albania moving towards a stabilisation and association agreement - without a stabilisation of the situation in the former yugoslav republic of macedonia and without integrated models in the other areas of the former yugoslavia, it will not be possible to find autonomous solutions for kosovo. it is the overall context of the region and the overall context of the implementation of the stability pact that will allow the situation in kosovo to be resolved.

counsel. - (pt) the charter of fundamental rights is one of the most promising elements of all our institutional work in the framework of the european union. as you know, the charter was born out of the decision of the european council in cologne. it is an element which seems to us to be fundamental to the constitution of what can be considered a kind of ethical pillar of the european union and what can be considered a need for the european union, on the path to a union of values, to strengthen its dimension not only at the internal level but also to legitimise its own assertion at the external level on the basis of a set of common values. this is of course of great importance in the union of fifteen. four or five months ago we probably did not think that this was so important for the european union of 15. fortunately, today we think differently, at least some of us. this is also a very important issue in the enlarged union, particularly in terms of future political cultures. as for the content of the charter, as you know, it is still being discussed. there have been only two meetings of the group responsible for its preparation, now called the convention for the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. several problems must be raised on this basis. first, we must determine whether it is a declaratory text or a binding text. secondly, the question of the content of the charter: whether it is a binding document or not, the problem of content will still arise, namely whether it is an accumulation of principles which the member states of the union defend and which they have enshrined at the level of their internal constitutional systems or whether it is a new corpus which could be in some way the common matrix of the union. secondly, there is a fundamental, present problem which is more in the question and which seems to me of the utmost importance, but which has not been solved. the question is whether the charter applies to citizens of the union or to thirdcountry nationals residing within the union. this is also an open question. finally, there is the crucial problem of the subordination of legal systems, of the compatibility between the legal system of the court of strasbourg and the legal order of the court of luxembourg. this problem must be solved in the definition of the charter of fundamental rights. you also mention the question of social rights. of course, we must reach a common ground here. as the portuguese delegation, i would say that we consider economic and social rights to be an essential heritage of the european union which must be part of any charter of the european union and any corpus of values of the european union. i don't know if everyone shares that idea. as regards the last question, there is the problem of equality between men and women within the european union. i am convinced that this is one of the aspects which will certainly receive the greatest attention in this charter, especially since we ourselves had a doctrine on this subject in the discussion of the amsterdam treaty, which was expressed in the amsterdam treaty and which represented a qualitative leap forward compared to maastricht. finally, as regards the incorporation of the charter into the treaty on european union, i am currently chairing a working group responsible for leading the preparatory group for the intergovernmental conference. the presidency intends to ensure that as soon as the charter has been agreed by the people responsible for drawing it up - as you know the charter is outside the council, it is a convention with an elected president, representatives of the european parliament, the national parliaments and governments of the member states and the european commission - as soon as this charter is ready (this is a process which we do not control, which we are only accompanying through a vice-president of the convention, who is also the representative of the presidency of the european union in this group), we think it would be desirable for it to be part of the new treaty on european union and for this matter to be immediately transferred to the intergovernmental conference.

- (el) mr president, i am neither a member of the legal affairs committee nor a lawyer; however, like almost all my colleagues, i understand that it is essential that the single european economic area should have its counterpart in a single area of law, freedom and security. in this context, the issues of judicial cooperation in criminal matters are important. what have i understood from the texts i have read? that it is dangerous to rely on council regulations. there are contradictions, differing opinions on the definition of europe, different justice systems, which not only create confusion and inefficiency, but also dangerous situations. let us take article 18, which has attracted the attention of many colleagues, and which allows every country, every large country, if you will, every country with the technical means to do so, to 'penetrate' another state and monitor its telecommunications, without its approval, moreover. i think that the legal affairs committee and the rapporteur, mr di pietro, have done a very interesting job. it is a pity that mr di pietro's very condensed statement of reasons could only be presented in the meagre five minutes he had here. despite this, we have made progress in the defence of the rights of the states and the accused and the citizens, a path which i think needs to be much more integrated and much bolder. i would, however, draw your attention to the fact that all this is taking place in a context of confusion and schizophrenia in criminal law. a child, a student, a pupil who smokes hashish will be treated in one country in an educational way, and in another, he will be considered a criminal and locked up in the nurseries of crime that are penitentiaries and prisons. how can there be criminal cooperation in an environment like this? in conclusion, i believe that this is why we must make progress, particularly in the area of harmonising the law, where it is not with organised criminal activities, but with criminal activities of citizens, which are now social phenomena.

of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities, i would like to share with you three basic elements which, in my view, determine the present and future of european tourism in an international environment. in europe, the tourism market now contributes 5.5% of gross national product and absorbs a fairly large percentage, approximately 6%, of the labour force. these figures are incomparably higher for some countries. to understand the importance of tourism for certain parts of europe, it should be noted that in recent years in my country, greece, a country of tourism, we have seen an ageing of the rural population and an over-concentration in the large urban centres. the only regions where the population of middle-aged people is retreating are those which have developed tourism. it is therefore clear that tourism is not only a european economic issue but also a priority for the economic and social participation of the regions of the european union. the explosion of the world market is another factor. ten billion tourists are expected by 2010. unfortunately, the trend in europe is the opposite. the shares of our countries are falling dramatically each year to the benefit of asia, the far east and america. the tourist market is directly and daily affected by the liberalization of transport, the fall in costs and the competitiveness of new tourist centres beyond europe. in addition, 90% of the enterprises active in tourism are small and medium-sized enterprises: these enterprises, which constitute the vital cell of our social and economic life, are faced with new obstacles and must respond to the challenges of this global evolution. all this is of particular importance, as the rapporteur has also said, when tourism is assessed as a single productive system because of its specific nature and because it absorbs large quantities of agricultural and industrial products, services and transport and creates a large mass of added value. i would point out in this connection that international competition is fierce and that in order to overcome it successfully we must consider and pool a long-term strategy. the need to develop human resources in tourism to meet the new technological challenges of our time must be taken very seriously. today, no one can ignore the fact that new digital technologies, with the internet at the forefront, not to mention mobile telephony and digital television, are radically changing tourism as a whole, as well as its partial structures and business models. the introduction of these new technologies is not a threat but a new opportunity for our tourism because they give the opportunity to develop innovative tourism products and services that our tourism needs for a long period of time, in order to attract new customers and offer new forms of tourism. new technologies also open up new markets to traditionally undervalued, non-tourist regions, which can be developed into tourist centres and which can be the subject of major investment. the programmes of tourist schools and of personnel training within companies, as well as the training and reintegration programmes for the unemployed, should also be adapted to this new labour market. the committee on women's rights has dealt particularly with the issue of education, because women constitute 53% - according to official statistics - of workers in the tourism sector and, at the same time, constitute the majority of workers in the tourism sector, who are vulnerable to social and economic changes. it should be borne in mind that these workers, and in particular women, face special problems such as the issue of seasonal work, the need for renewal of contracts from one year to the next, social protection, and the lack of interest in continuing training and reduced opportunities for advancement. we therefore ask the european commission to take special measures, but also measures to integrate the principle of equal opportunities into all tourism and business development programmes.
- (el) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we are dealing today with a very important sector, which covers more than 10 million direct jobs in the european union and which helps the most and least favoured, island and remote regions to stand on their feet. it is clear that tourism creates additional jobs, contributing to the strengthening of employment, especially for the most vulnerable groups in society: women and young people. in this context, the absence of a european policy on tourism is, i believe, a serious contradiction. the absence of a legal basis makes it almost impossible to take action in this area. the occasional use of article 308 - the old article 235 - which requires unanimity, has resulted in a programme of ecu 8 million for 5 years and 15 member states not even being adopted. the measures which have been taken occasionally in the past have been fragmentary and therefore ineffective; the commission has also taken the view that the rules on the free movement of goods and services are not compatible with the common market, and that the rules on the free movement of goods and services are not compatible with the common market. i would point out that unfortunately, in the context of the restructuring of the commission's departments, tourism is now only a part, not even a department, of the directorate-general for enterprise. we must defend our tourism! the guidelines which we believe can form the basis for its support are the following: capital injection for tourism in preparation for the new revision which will constitute a legal basis for the sector; establishment of a community tourism policy which will support the sector without disrupting the functioning of the tourism market; coordination of tourism policy and parallel community policies which influence it directly or indirectly; a study of the sector and long- or medium-term measures to support the competitiveness of the tourism product. mr president, commissioner, since we are talking about employment, i should like to mention the very serious subject of the bankruptcies of tour operators and their consequences, mainly for hotels which are small and mediumsized enterprises, and the lack of the necessary organization and access to information to ascertain the solvency of the other contracting party. it is obvious that action at national level is not possible. on the other hand, at european level, various measures could be considered to improve relations between hotels and tour operators, such as a regulation governing their relations, the promotion of a special bankruptcy fund, etc., in cooperation with representatives of both sectors. in the past, the commission has requested quantitative information on the damage suffered by firms as a result of bankruptcies in order to obtain an opinion from the european confederations of hotel and catering and travel agencies, hotrec and ectaa, and to develop and draft a regulation governing their relations. since then, however, no progress has been made. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality and competitiveness of european tourism, but also to ensure that businesses can continue to operate as far as possible. they must also be aimed at the rational use of natural and cultural resources and tourist infrastructure, thus contributing to the sustainable and balanced development of tourism. in conclusion, i would like to congratulate mr viceconte and, as a cretan resident of a tourist region, i would ask you to consider tourism not only as a pleasant holiday but also as a job creator for a europe that needs it.

the commission's report is a report on the economic situation in the community, and it is a report on the economic situation in the community. the background to this picture must be stagnation, with annual growth of only 1.9% in the 1990s, despite some cases of higher growth during that decade, but which we have not been able to consolidate, according to your rapporteur. it follows that if we do not want the same failures as we have seen in the past to happen again, we must use the current window of opportunity for improving economic performance to launch a new policy of transition to higher and more self-sustaining levels of growth in our economy. that's the challenge. in the face of this challenge, the commission has essentially recommended that our economic policy should simply continue our past position of simply implementing the monetary and fiscal fundamentals of the economy properly, relying for the rest on the miracle of markets without further intervention by conscious social actors. in europe, according to your rapporteur, this is exactly the attitude that has failed us in the past, not just once but repeatedly. such an attitude carries downside risks which i understood the ecofin council was discussing at the moment. these risks include a correction in the us economy and the future trend in oil prices and longterm interest rates in the eu. if one of these risks were to materialise in a negative way, what would be our response - more laissez-faire or would we be better prepared than during the previous downturn? i have proposed that parliament should recommend an active economic policy consisting of two main elements. firstly, we must adopt the excellent and outstanding econometric analysis of the commission and ask the council to translate it into an action programme comprising certain quantifiable and interconnected objectives in terms of the level of growth of investment and employment which would make our commitment to a policy of full employment operational and credible. a central component of such a programme would be to increase the level of investment in capital formation from the 19% it fell to during the decade to at least 23% to 24% of gdp, both in the private and public sectors. in order to allow the public sector to play the significant and necessary leading role in investment policy, the council would have to sacrifice some of its most sacred cows. it should reconsider its negative attitude towards the major infrastructure programmes of the transeuropean networks, which have been gathering dust in the drawers for too long, and allow their financing at an adequate scale through european community securities and accept an interpretation of the concept of budget deficits which allows governments to borrow for investment purposes without going against the restrictions of the stability pact. that was the first pillar of my recommendation. the second pillar consisted of the adoption of a large number of structural reforms, privatisation, proposals for deregulation and suggestions to facilitate the transition to a knowledge-based economy, which, as we know, forms the backbone of the presidency's proposals for the lisbon summit. experts have repeatedly stated that the structural reform programme, combined with an expansionist investment and growth programme, is capable of pulling the european economy out of its state of inertia, whereas unilateral policies are doomed to failure. unfortunately, my colleagues in the economic affairs committee, with the help and encouragement of the liberals, found my approach unacceptable. i am glad that after five years of insistence they have finally found the full employment objective acceptable, but i must say that merely referring to full employment without proposing a concrete programme to achieve the objectives is too much of a statement of intent and too unconvincing to serve as the political message of optimism that people have been expecting from us for too long in my view. it is certainly not my place to judge the alliances that other people have made but if the people's party and the liberals are so desperate to cement their alliance, let them at least propose something positive that has a chance of working and not repeat the same old rants or ideas that have failed so singularly in the past, nor thwart the concrete efforts of others for the sake of opposing them. i believe that the political message of this parliament should be something more original than a mere resurrection of the neoclassical economic mantra. something more solid than a call to submit to the fatalities of the market economy, something more decent than the opposition to worthy objectives and the refusal to grant them the means for their achievement. the report of the economic committee which is before you fails in all these respects. unless it is restored to its original form by parliament, accepting the amendments tabled by the socialist group again, i am obliged to ask my socialist colleagues - which is unusual but unavoidable - and all those who think that parliament should speak a clear language, to vote against this report which bears my name but which no longer belongs to me in thought or in conscience.
